# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-21

| **[Manga2Animate: An Agentic Framework for Faithful Manga Animation with Script and Storyboard Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting existing manga into coherent animation that stays faithful to the source is a valuable yet challenging task. Existing methods either access the manga mainly through text, losing its characters and art style, or feed manga pages directly into video generation models, which misinterpret the manga's structure and produce incorrect storylines. In this paper, we propose Manga2Animate, a multi-agent framework that grounds every stage of generation in the source manga. We decompose the task into two intermediate representations, an animation script and a visual storyboard. Each representation is produced by a creator agent and reviewed against the source panels by a paired reviewer agent. We tailor representation-specific selection and dual-level feedback to progressively refine both before synthesizing the final animation. Extensive experiments and a user study demonstrate that Manga2Animate produces animations more faithful to the source manga and more consistent across shots than state-of-the-art approaches. Project page: https://annchenn.github.io/Manga2Animate/</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://annchenn.github.io/Manga2Animate/</p></details> |
| **[WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26847v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 14 figures, 12 tables, including references and appendices</p></details> |
| **[Train An Imaginative Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26845v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A seemingly reasonable push in Sokoban can make the puzzle unsolvable. To avoid such mistakes, an agent has to anticipate the consequences of its actions before execution, especially when feedback from the real environment is sparse or actions are irreversible. We introduce Agentic Imagination (Agima), a training framework that trains an agent to imagine before acting, i.e. to be imaginative. Agima trains a single omni model to propose actions, generate images of their outcomes, and then reason over these imagined futures to decide what to execute in the real environment. Rather than committing to a single plan, the agent can simulate a sequence of actions, explore alternatives, and compare possible futures. We first use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to unify policy and world modeling within a single omni model. This enables the agent to predict future outcomes and perform basic forms of imagination, such as forward thinking and counterfactual branching. We then use agentic reinforcement learning (RL) to teach the agent to reason with its imagined futures and use them effectively for planning. Across four environments spanning Maze2D, Sokoban, Pacman, and PushT, Agima generalizes to harder settings unseen during training. Trained with SFT alone on two-box Sokoban, Agima solves 40% of four-box puzzles while seeing the real board only once every five moves, compared with an action-only planner that only solves 2%. After RL, new behaviors emerge in how Agima uses its imagination: it learns a new unified format that was unseen during the SFT stage, learns to conduct forward thinking only when necessary, and branches more frequently at critical decision points. An agent that can picture where its actions lead can learn when and where to look ahead, that is, to plan. Project page: https://agima.net/.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.</p></details> | 15 pages, 4 figures |
| **[AGR: Agentic Generative Retrieval with Tool-Augmented Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26831v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large-scale search and recommendation systems derive much of their retrieval quality from rich, heterogeneous features, yet existing generative retrieval (GR) methods struggle to exploit such feature ecosystems efficiently. Static feature injection either encodes all available signals or places them according to predetermined schemes, increasing serving cost and introducing irrelevant or conflicting evidence. More fundamentally, existing methods cannot adaptively determine when a feature is needed, which feature to select, or where it should influence hierarchical Semantic ID (SID) generation. We propose Agentic Generative Retrieval (AGR), a tool-augmented paradigm that interleaves SID generation with on-demand feature acquisition. Conditioned on the request and decoded SID prefix, AGR decides whether to invoke a tool, which feature to acquire, and where its observation should influence subsequent decoding, thereby steering generation toward feature-consistent SID branches. To supervise this behavior, we construct tool-augmented trajectories based on feature applicability and information gain along the SID hierarchy, and train the model through SID pretraining followed by tool-augmented fine-tuning. Experiments on an industrial dataset and three public benchmarks, together with an online A/B test, consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of AGR.</p></details> |  |
| **[ProteinGeoBench: Frontier Structural-Biology Agents Are Fluent at Lookup but Lost in Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26829v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier agents retrieve structural metadata well and compute geometry from coordinates less reliably. We ask how much of a benchmark score reflects that gap and how much reflects the evaluation configuration around the model. We study ProteinGeoBench, a suite of 131 protein-structure tasks, using 4,249 rollouts from four frontier models and 4,486 further trials across 26 models. Three of the four frontier models fall within 0.031 of one another (0.785 to 0.816) with no difference between them resolved, while the fourth scores 0.707 and all three gaps to it exclude zero. Three of the four do better on metadata lookup than on numeric calculation. The fourth is slightly better on numeric. Numeric tasks are 28 of the 131 but half of the twelve tasks that all four models fail. The ranking also depends on the evaluation budget: with one run per task, 23.9% of bootstrap replicates put a different model first. On a selected 15-task slice, removing network access lowers scores more than blocking eight structural-database domains, but the two contrasts were graded in different passes and cannot be cleanly decomposed. Swapping in each model's own command-line agent changes scores by -0.071 to +0.086, a comparison too small to rule out moderate harness effects. Across 26 models, numeric performance rises with non-numeric performance rather than staying flat. The experiments use different task subsets, so they cannot rank model, harness, access and task composition against one another. Evaluations should report results by task family, along with the number of runs, the harness and the access conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures, 10 tables. Benchmark and tasks: https://github.com/Protege-AI/ProteinGeoBench</p></details> |
| **[Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26828v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM.</p></details> | 35 pages |
| **[RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://wangyuchi369.github.io/refcraft/</p></details> |
| **[Accountable partial understanding: securing the epistemic attack surface of agentic systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26799v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Attackers profit from what defenders do not understand. Software security has treated the gap between system behavior and human comprehension as a condition to narrow through analysis and assurance, booking the remainder as accepted risk. Agentic AI systems change that gap: when software reads, writes, explains, and authorizes security-relevant behavior, partial understanding moves faster, grows more opaque, and couples to delegated authority. This paper characterizes the resulting epistemic attack surface and presents a taxonomy of four interacting attack categories, which target the interpretation of intent, the production and consumption of evidence, the scope of delegated authority, and the comprehension of human supervisors. It then defines accountable partial understanding (APU) and specifies six necessary conditions: bounded scope with explicit assumptions, inspectable evidence, assigned responsibility, visible residual unknowns, actionability, and temporal maintenance. It explains why delegating interpretation to software agents differs from distributing knowledge among people and distinguishes APU from risk acceptance, explainable AI, assurance cases, formal verification, and human-in-the-loop oversight. From the conditions, the paper derives five design principles: bounded delegation, decoupled evidence, assumption tracking with invalidation, role-specific accountability views, and intervention-preserving design with residual unknown registers. It introduces two measurable probe dimensions, evidence linkage and invalidation latency, for testing whether accountability mechanisms hold under change and adversarial pressure. Three running examples (an AI coding agent, an autonomous cyber-defense agent, and an AI-mediated insulin delivery update) ground the analysis. Security without full understanding is already here; the task is to make it accountable.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 1 table. Under review</p></details> |
| **[Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Market Making on Prediction-Market Order Books](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26789v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A market maker provides liquidity by quoting prices to buy and sell an asset, earning the spread while managing inventory risk. We study market makers trained with multi-agent reinforcement learning on prediction markets, specifically Polymarket, whose thin order books pose distinct challenges. Three challenges underpin this research: adverse selection may cause a policy trained on real data to stop quoting; the consequences of a quote can arrive many steps after the action; and prediction-market L2 data does not identify individual orders needed for simulation. We address these challenges by reconstructing order-level LOBSTER-format data from Polymarket and training agents in JaxMARL-HFT on 417 real markets. We enrich the baseline market maker's observation space with the book and recent flow, redesign its actions and inventory controls, and propose a cooperative multi-agent decomposition into a quoter and a risk manager trained using recurrent Independent Proximal Policy Optimisation (IPPO) with RNN policies. We investigate reward shaping, particularly reward blending and post-fill markout, to mitigate credit-assignment difficulties between the agents. Our redesigned single-agent market maker quotes more frequently and incurs smaller held-out losses than the retrained JaxMARL-HFT baseline, although the contributions of jointly changed observations, actions and risk constraints are not separately identified. Decomposition alone performs poorly; careful reward design substantially improves the desk relative to the unshaped split and yields our best result. Models are evaluated on realised end-of-episode portfolio value on held-out markets. None establishes positive average portfolio value in periodic held-out evaluation, and each configuration was trained with one seed. The environment, data and protocol lay a foundation for makers that quote many linked markets at once.</p></details> | <details><summary>50 pa...</summary><p>50 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables. Extended version of a paper accepted at ICAIF 2026 Workshop on AI-Driven Market Microstructure. MSc dissertation, London School of Economics and Political Science (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Topological Sheaves of Irreducibility: A Theoretical Framework for Multi-Agent Task Obstructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26777v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems can differ under identical agents and communication budgets when they support different forms of joint interaction. We introduce Topological Sheaves of Irreducibility (TSI), a sheaf-theoretic framework separating task-level compatibility obstructions from system capability. A task is represented by an interaction complex $K$ and a capacity sheaf $\mathcal{F}$; nonzero $H^n(K;\mathcal{F})$ defines a structural signature, while registered interventions test capability. For cocycle data, cochain realization is solvable exactly when the represented cohomology class vanishes. Examples show that the signature is sheaf-relative, and the degree-1 reduction yields cycle-space witnesses and observation requirements. We evaluate two finite one-round models. Across 15,625 degree-1 inputs, an all-agent additive primitive solves decision and repair exactly; the optimal pairwise system reaches 88.80% on decision, one input above the 88.79% majority-class baseline, and 26.63% on repair. On a degree-2 tetrahedral-boundary task over 531,441 inputs, the same-2-skeleton system retains all four three-agent primitives yet matches the pairwise-only optimum throughout the registered separation window, whereas adding the class-filling four-agent primitive makes both tasks exact. Status-preserving robustness checks retain exact performance but are not cost-matched to the class-restoring ablation. Conditional on the registered primitives and cost models, TSI provides task-relative, falsifiable tests of higher-order interaction.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 1 figure, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[DashAct: A Progressive Diagnostic Benchmark for GUI Agents in Interactive Dashboard Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32385v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive dashboards require users to reveal and connect evidence across stateful interactions. Although graphical user interface (GUI) agents could automate this process, existing dashboard benchmarks primarily report final answers or task success. They provide limited insight into whether failures arise from maintaining the analytical process, selecting actions, or grounding visual targets. We introduce DashAct, a progressive diagnostic benchmark that evaluates the same annotated dashboard interactions under systematically varied contextual and spatial support. DashAct contains 348 human-verified interaction trajectories with milestone dependencies and hierarchical target annotations. Its three stages evaluate end-to-end execution, verified-context action prediction, and target-conditioned grounding under four visual conditions. Aligned action and spatial annotations connect outcomes across these settings, providing diagnostic evidence for interpreting online failures. Experiments reveal persistent difficulties in action prediction and fine-grained grounding, alongside model-dependent responses to contextual and spatial support. A Qwen-based adaptation study further improves target localization and online Milestone Coverage, illustrating how DashAct can guide practical agent improvements.</p></details> |  |
| **[StepBench: Diagnosing Real-World Procedural Understanding in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26679v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating an agent's planning ability remains challenging for open-domain tasks, where verifying procedures through execution is often impractical. Existing evaluations typically assess only the final plan and do not distinguish procedural errors such as missing, incorrect, or misordered actions. We introduce STEPBENCH, a benchmark of 6,885 verified procedures crawled from wikiHow across 81 open-domain categories. From each procedure, we derive 5 diagnostic tasks: four with deterministic answers involving withheld, incorrect, or shuffled steps, and one requiring next-step generation. We additionally evaluate full-plan generation to examine how diagnostic performance correlates with end-to-end plan validity. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. Across 14 configurations of 12 models, we find substantial differences across planning capabilities, with step ordering proving the most difficult: the strongest model correctly orders only 63.0% of procedures, dropping to 33.1% for those with nine or more steps. Incorrect-step detection also predicts judged plan validity across models, suggesting a lower-cost alternative for evaluating generation quality. On STEPBENCH-Val, comprising 214 procedures published in 2026, comprehension scores and rankings remain stable, suggesting limited influence from memorisation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Preli...</summary><p>Preliminary version accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Resource-Aware Agentic AI (RAAAI)</p></details> |
| **[PACT: Plan-Augmented Coordination via Trace-Graphs for Dynamic Multi-Agent Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26671v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent approaches have been investigated recently as a means of improving the scalability of reinforcement learning for neural combinatorial optimization. Existing methods assume that all problem instance information is known upfront. However, real-world problems are often dynamic: relevant information is progressively revealed as the agents take actions (e.g., arriving at an edge in a routing problem only to discover it is untraversable). In such cases, existing methods fail to coordinate agents to explore and exploit the progressively revealed information. To address the arising coordination challenge, we introduce PACT (Plan-Augmented Coordination via Trace-graphs). PACT augments the environment graph with auxiliary nodes representing the plans of agents, which are connected to the resource nodes to indicate future dependencies. The proposed message-passing scheme over the augmented graph grounds inter-agent coordination in the environment and enables proactive, rather than reactive, conflict resolution. We show that PACT improves solution quality in dynamic problems relative to baselines and exhibits the best transfer to larger instances on which, remarkably, it outperforms centralized oracles with privileged access to instance uncertainty.</p></details> |  |
| **[Honeypots for AI Agent Insider Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26664v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents deployed inside organizations can pose an insider threat if they make mistakes, are compromised by an attacker, or pursue misaligned objectives. We propose honeypots as an AI control method to improve the security of internal agent deployments. Rather than only using honeypots for pre-deployment evaluations, defenders should plant decoy opportunities for harmful actions throughout the production environment to detect misbehavior, incriminate malicious agents, and deter attacks. Such honeypots can elicit misbehavior under real deployment conditions, identify malicious agents even if chain-of-thought monitorability degrades, provide reliable warnings about agent misbehavior to security teams, and scale cheaply to many agent instances. The presence of honeypots also introduces uncertainty about which opportunities are real, thus deterring malicious agents from attacking and reducing the rate of misbehavior. We discuss the limitations of honeypots and give reasons to expect them to remain effective as agent capabilities increase. Finally, we outline key research questions to measure and improve the effectiveness of honeypots: testing the robustness of honeypots, comparing their detection effectiveness with other monitoring methods, understanding their incrimination and deterrence effects, and building supporting infrastructure.</p></details> |  |
| **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 3rd Workshop on LLMs and Generative AI in Finance (AI4F) at ACM ICAIF 2026 (non-archival). 11 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Code: https://github.com/dukesky/allpath-trading-agent</p></details> |
| **[Tandem: Serving Hybrid Linear-Attention GUI Agents on Mobile NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26660v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Smartphone GUI agents complete tasks in mobile apps by repeatedly reading the screen and asking a model for the next action. Mobile neural processing units (NPUs) enable local execution, keeping screen contents on the phone, but model latency accumulates with every action. Hybrid linear attention models reduce memory requirements, yet reusing their recurrent state requires checkpoints at the appropriate input positions. Agent requests retain instructions but rewrite recent history, so checkpoint policies designed for chat continuation can miss reusable prefixes. Retaining rollback states for speculative decoding can also move prompt processing off the NPU's matrix unit, adding overhead that outweighs decoding savings on short action outputs. A loaded model can further hold the NPU awake between tasks. We present Tandem, a runtime for hybrid GUI agents on mobile NPUs. Tandem saves recurrent state where consecutive prompts diverge, allowing later requests to reuse previously processed context. It keeps most prefill on the matrix unit and produces rollback states through a short per-token tail. Its verification kernel reads each weight block once for all proposed tokens. Between request bursts, Tandem releases NPU power votes without unloading the model. We implement Tandem in llama.cpp and evaluate it on recorded agent interactions across two Snapdragon generations. On a Snapdragon 8 Elite, Tandem reduces per-step latency by 30% for text-based screen descriptions and 16% for screenshots relative to llama.cpp's fastest NPU configuration, reduces energy per text step by 33%, and lowers standby power with the model loaded from 0.76 to 0.22 W.</p></details> |  |
| **[SpatialScout: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore and Map 3D Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26659v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial intelligence requires agents to actively acquire visual evidence and integrate observations into a coherent representation of the three-dimensional world. Yet spatial question answering offers limited evidence of whether models can construct accurate metric maps through autonomous exploration. We introduce SpatialScout, a benchmark for evaluating vision-language model (VLM) agents on active object-level 3D mapping. SpatialScout comprises 80 diverse indoor scenes containing 3,140 target objects. Given an object inventory, RGB observations, camera calibration, and camera poses, agents control a camera to explore each scene and estimate object centers in a shared coordinate frame. The task requires coordinating viewpoint selection, cross-view association, geometric estimation, and map revision under bounded interaction budgets. Evaluation jointly measures localization accuracy and target coverage through object-scale overlap scores that penalize missing predictions. Experiments with frontier VLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated model, Claude Opus 5.5, achieves a mean overlap score of 41.71%, with 47.23% of targets exceeding an intersection-over-union (IoU) threshold of 0.5 and only 3.25% exceeding 0.9. These results reveal a substantial gap between recovering coarse object layouts and constructing complete, precise metric maps. SpatialScout provides a quantitative testbed for developing agents that build reliable 3D spatial representations through active perception. Code is available at https://github.com/JesseZZZZZ/SpatialScout.</p></details> |  |
| **[From TCP/IP to MCP: Security and Governance Lessons from Internet Protocols for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26624v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The past two years have seen considerable interest in protocols for managing how AI agents interact with human users, external tools, and each other. In many ways, these protocols, including the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Agent2Agent (A2A), mirror the suite of protocols that underlie the modern internet. Motivated by this parallel, this paper draws lessons from the development, history, and governance of internet protocols with the aim of informing the responsible development of AI agent protocols. Based on our analysis, we make two conclusions. First, if AI agent protocols do not sufficiently prioritise security in the early stages of development, they risk accumulating a security deficit that becomes increasingly costly to address over time due to practical and economic challenges. Second, current governance structures for AI agent protocols centralise decision-making power in a small number of individuals and companies, raising important questions regarding legitimacy, especially if they become critical digital infrastructure. We conclude with a discussion and implications for policymakers and developers of AI agent protocols.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? A Tool-Call Vector Shaped by Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09624v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool calling, invoking external tools on demand, is central to agentic LLMs, yet the mechanism that decides whether a model calls a tool or responds directly remains poorly understood. Agentic prompts are long and heavily scaffolded, combining role instructions, tool schemas, format templates, and the user's request across hundreds of tokens, creating a noisy, highly entangled context in which no single controllable variable for mechanistic analysis is obvious. To obtain such a variable, we propose a method that converts complex agentic prompts into minimal contrastive pairs in which a single request verb determines the tool-call decision: replacing an execution-verb (e.g., \textit{write}) with an analysis-verb (e.g., \textit{discuss}) reliably flips the decision, suggesting it is mediated by a compact internal state. We construct 500 such paired prompts across Python, Java, and C++ (300 for mechanistic analysis, 200 held out for evaluation). We trace the decision to a vector, $μ_Δ$, that is both causally necessary and sufficient and generalizes beyond the discovery prompts to native multi-turn $τ^2$-Bench trajectories and verb-free requests. Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. | **[Connection Escrow: Holding AI-Agent Pod Egress in the Kernel Until an Isolated Verifier Rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26501v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents in containers act by opening network connections, and the text that drives them can be hostile. The connection is released only on a signed verdict naming that one socket cookie and destination, unexpired and unused; no verdict by the deadline resets it, and a late approval is refused. The agent is unmodified. A 36-check adversarial suite with real cgroups passes on the eBPF hold point (33 of the checks also apply, and pass, on an iptables hold point): refused connections never reach the destination; forged, rebound, replayed, expired and late verdicts are refused; the agent's attempt to reach the verifier is itself escrowed; child-process and renamed-binary bypasses fail; UDP is pinned to the resolver. Twenty-three of 24 runs respected the position limit; logs identify the exception's size and duration. The same live data produced different results across assigned strategies. However, by treating each action step as an independent trial, they overlook trajectory structure and become fragile when trajectories are perturbed, truncated, or observed without reliable alignment. | **[Envy Cycle Elimination with Strategic Agents: Best Responses and Fairness Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31253v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With strong evidence in the literature showing that fairness and truthfulness are incompatible, there is a recent line of work focusing on the fairness properties of equilibria of simple fair division mechanisms, especially Round-Robin. We consider the Envy Cycle Elimination (E-C-E) procedure of Lipton et al. EC 2024, one of the most versatile tools in fair division. While this simple and intuitive algorithm achieves allocations that are envy-free up to one item (EF1) for any number of agents and general monotone valuation functions, surprisingly little is known about its behavior when agents act strategically. We demonstrate how the presence of incentives, although highly natural and relevant for the majority of applications, completely removes the intuitive clarity in the algorithm's execution, even for a few agents and very simple valuation functions. Additionally, while in the standard algorithmic setting there is great flexibility in how the details of E-C-E are implemented, here additional specifications are needed before the procedure is clearly defined, the choice of which has a potentially huge impact on the agents' behavior. Despite these obstacles, for various natural versions of E-C-E, we give the first results on the existence of Pure Nash Equilibria of the resulting mechanisms, and show there exist versions where fairness guarantees are approximately preserved for agents who play best responses\newx{, as long as there are at most three agents; for four or more agents, no such guarantee can hold for every best response, even at a pure Nash equilibrium. | **[Bidirectionality is Necessary in Cellular Machines for Exact-Arrival Multi-Agent Path Finding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26272v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On ranked directed acyclic graphs coupled by vertex conflicts we study exact-arrival multi-agent path finding (departure and arrival pinned in place and time) posed as a distributed output task: every cell must emit its own bit of a valid joint path-support mask. The machines are cellular: one automaton per cell, state factored over cells, communication only along a fixed local fabric, synchronous rounds, a query-independent output round, no central decoder, and a per-topology transition code. "Necessary" here means: on an explicit hard topology, over all its feasible queries, no universally correct local-only architecture can renounce either temporal orientation; with global flags, impossibility becomes a bandwidth-round trade-off. The class is not vacuous: a flooding machine solves every instance in as many rounds as the diameter of the local fabric. The bound survives two concessions: we supply every agent's exact individual cavity, which alone solves the single-agent problem in zero rounds and makes the single-agent direction theorem vacuous; and we allow $k$ one-bit global aggregate flags. On a twelve-cell, two-agent, depth-three topology no forward-only machine with $k=0$ is correct on two feasible queries, for any number of rounds; the time-reversed topology does the same to backward-only machines. Placing $p$ copies side by side gives, for forward-only machines, $J_R+kR\ge p$ at constant depth, where $J_R$ measures how much of the query reaches the targets' cones and is $0$ here. A backward-only machine solves that family in constant rounds and a flag-streaming one in $\lceil p/k\rceil+O(1)$, so the bound is tight in order; on the composite family neither orientation can be renounced. The finite lemmas of the gadget and the pair-invariance contradictions are checked in Lean 4; the semantic bridge, the cone lemma and the counting are paper proofs. We report no benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages. Evaluations on two automated data science benchmarks demonstrate that AutoMind delivers superior performance versus state-of-the-art baselines. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. Combining \falcon{} with either proxy reward further improves held-out performance, suggesting that execution-grounded feedback can mitigate reward overoptimization while preserving useful guidance from existing security signals.</p></details> |  |
| **[SWE-Residual: Evaluating Starting-State Robustness of Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25899v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering (SWE) benchmarks are the primary way to measure the capabilities of coding agents: an agent is placed in a code repository or machine environment, given a request, and judged by hidden tests. These benchmarks almost always start the agent from a clean state that contains no earlier work on the task. In practice, however, agents often continue work that a developer or another agent has already done and reported as complete, with no indication of what remains incorrect or missing. We introduce SWE-Residual, a benchmark for Starting-State Robustness: the ability to maintain performance on a task when the starting state already contains earlier work on that task. SWE-Residual contains 198 Residual States drawn from 111 tasks in DeepSWE and Terminal-Bench, where the earlier work modifies a code repository or a whole machine environment. Each Residual State is the state a predecessor agent left behind after submitting its work as complete, although the hidden tests still fail. The evaluated agent receives only the original request and this state, without the predecessor's trajectory or any sign that the attempt failed; because the request and hidden tests are unchanged, the starting state is the only difference from the source benchmark. Across five frontier agents, success drops by 20.6--53.5 percentage points on DeepSWE and 20.6--34.9 points on Terminal-Bench relative to a Clean Start, and agent rankings on DeepSWE reverse. Failed continuations show a pattern we call acceptance bias: agents treat the inherited work as the definition of what remains to be done instead of checking it against the request. On DeepSWE, in 58.9--88.2% of failed runs, they submit with exactly the same tests failing, although they pass most of these tests from a Clean Start. SWE-Residual thus exposes a capability gap that clean-start evaluation does not capture.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[When Should AI Chime In? Benchmarking Timely Contribution of Proactive Agents in Multi-Party Chat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25896v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Proactive agents can support group collaboration by surfacing useful information without waiting to be asked, but even relevant contributions can be disruptive if poorly timed. In this work, we isolate the timing question: given a fixed candidate contribution such as a clarification or relevant fact, when should an AI agent chime in? We introduce PITCH (Proactive Intervention Timing in CHat), to our knowledge the first benchmark with large-scale human judgments of proactive agent timing collected from participants in the same unscripted multi-party conversations they took part in. To collect high-quality judgments, we design a novel interface in which 2-4 people chat synchronously, author candidate AI contribution messages, place their own and others' contributions into the chat history, and then deliberate as a group about the best placement for each message. PITCH contains 384 candidate contributions and 1,091 judgments, each marking an ideal turn, an acceptable window, or that a contribution should not appear, from 89 participants engaged in 35 conversations. Evaluating 15 models across 28 configurations, we find that while models can place contributions well when given the entire conversation history, they systematically chime in too early when asked to decide live as the conversation unfolds: best-turn accuracy falls from 57.8% to 19.0%, with nearly half of placements occurring before every participant's acceptable window. In comparison, lay humans deciding live are more accurate (23.7% vs 19.0%), and fewer of their placements are premature (39.7% vs 46.4%). Model reasoning traces show that 93% of intervention justifications cite relevance, while almost none consider waiting. By providing a human-grounded benchmark, our work reveals gaps in agents' ability to integrate into group settings and points towards proactive agents that can reason explicitly about appropriate timing.</p></details> |  |
| **[Inflak: A Unified Orchestration Architecture for Human-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21972v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-powered agents increasingly support open-ended activities, yet orchestrating when and how agents interact with humans remains challenging. Existing approaches often provide interaction mechanisms tied to particular tasks or execution patterns, lacking a unified architecture for diverse and evolving interaction needs. Its layered design with progressive resolution enables general applicability, runtime flexibility, module composability, and orchestration maintainability. We implement Inflak as agent-native, skill-based plugins within a general-purpose agent framework and evaluate it through a gallery of various application scenarios and interaction paradigms, technical performance testing, and a comparative implementation case study. The evaluations show that Inflak can serve as a general and extensible foundation for orchestrating diverse human-agent interaction flows.</p></details> |  |
| **[Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25858v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents are often deployed in environments where new knowledge, tools, and workflows keep arriving. Such an agent is therefore expected to adapt continually, learning new tasks as they arrive without forgetting earlier ones. This resembles the continual learning problem, but at a pace that conventional gradient updates cannot sustain. Nonparametric self-evolving agents are a natural alternative: they adapt by rewriting their own harness, such as prompts, memory, and skills, from task feedback while the model weights stay frozen. We study whether such agents are effective continual learners across four drifting task streams that shift in topic, workflow, or domain (built from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld). Evaluating three representative methods spanning experience retrieval and self-modification (ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness), we find that although self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, higher plasticity consistently comes at the cost of stability. Specifically, self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, interfering with unseen tasks and overwriting earlier knowledge as the stream shifts. Conversely, similarity-based memory retrieval localizes updates across distant domains, but still retrieves foreign memories on unseen tasks and suffers from retrieval collision when similar queries require conflicting workflows. These findings show that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners.</p></details> |  |
| **[MASIF: Implicit Failures for Process Observability in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25852v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems (MAS) can complete a task despite local execution errors that are corrected, bypassed, or carried forward without individually determining its outcome. We call these implicit failures (IFs) and study them as signals for process observability beyond final task outcome. Across 1,828 traces from nine MAS-benchmark configurations, IFs occur in 54.3% of successful and 75.2% of failed traces. Counterfactual replay further probes how repairing an IF changes the final outcome. To examine the practical value of IFs, we evaluate IF-based best-of-N trace selection against existing process reward models, and introduce a novel RL algorithm, IF-GRPO, that uses IF costs to refine preferences among successful trajectories in GRPO. Experiments show that IF-GRPO exhibits some gains in accuracy and process metrics. Importantly, it exposes trade-offs between task performance, IF count and cost, and token-normalized IF density. Overall, our results show that IFs provide complementary process observability into how MAS reach their outcomes, including error recovery, propagation, and IF-based process differences that final task success alone does not expose.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. However, effectively scheduling these topologies to dynamically determine who talks to whom and when to communicate remains a fundamental challenge in balancing collective reasoning performance against exponential token overhead. Existing topology scheduling approaches either enforce static communication graphs that cannot adapt to dynamic problem-solving states, or overlook agent role complementarity while failing to explicitly incorporate the synthesized graph structure into team-level decision evaluation. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Orchestrated within a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm, ACTS introduces a novel dual-attention mechanism. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. The project website is available at https://acts-mas.github.io/ACTS/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://acts-mas.github.io/ACTS/</p></details> |
| **[AURA-Eval: Evaluation Framework for Acting Under Risk Awareness in LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06783v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly taking actions with real consequences, yet safety evaluations often reduce their behavior to a final safe/unsafe outcome, obscuring whether the agent recognized the risk and how it responded. We introduce AURA-Eval, a framework for evaluating whether agents recognize contextual risk and adapt their behavior accordingly. AURA-Eval truncates tool-use trajectories at safety-critical decision points and asks the evaluated model to generate the next thought and action. It creates controlled scenario variations along six risk mechanisms: harm intensity, scale, target susceptibility, system dependency, oversight, and reversibility. It also creates paired scenarios that preserve the underlying goal but differ in whether safe fulfillment is available, allowing unsafe compliance and over-refusal to be evaluated together. An LLM-judge panel, validated against human annotations, evaluates each continuation along three axes: risk detection from the reasoning trace, and action type and safety from the action. From 157 R-Judge seed trajectories, we construct 1,249 evaluation items and evaluate 20 frontier, open-weight, and safety-tuned models. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. Controlled risk-mechanism analyses further show particularly high unsafe rates under changes to oversight and scale; removing pre-execution oversight flips 52.3% of cases that were safe on the corresponding original scenario to unsafe.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Finding to Fixing: How Useful is Fault Localization for Agentic Program Repair?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25826v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fault localization (FL) aims to identify code locations responsible for software failures and is commonly evaluated by how accurately it ranks known fault locations. Although recent LLM-based FL methods have improved localization accuracy, their impact on agentic repair remains unclear. To address this gap, we conduct a systematic empirical study of FL in agentic repair. We evaluate six recent FL methods on SWE-bench Verified and Defects4J with three LLMs under a common repair framework and analyze 16,248 repair trajectories. We also examine gold locations and bug-cause descriptions as forms of FL guidance across different repair contexts. The studied FL methods do not consistently improve repair success, and higher localization accuracy does not consistently lead to larger repair gains. Yet FL guides agents' early search and helps them reach relevant code sooner. Accurate locations can reduce repair token use by up to 39.2\%, but current FL methods often consume more tokens than they save during repair. Experiments with gold guidance suggest that FL has greater potential when bug inputs contain fewer location clues. Providing bug causes can further improve repair success beyond locations alone. We also find that ground truth from a single reference patch does not fully capture the locations used by successful repairs. These findings help clarify the value of FL in agentic repair and inform future work on its design and evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[AGORA: An Archive-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Workplace Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24526v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that reason over documents rather than answer from parametric knowledge. We study archive-grounded reasoning: locating sparse evidence across a large, messy collection of workplace files, reconciling inconsistent terminology, units, and time conventions, and computing an answer. Existing benchmarks address only parts of this setting and none jointly stresses archive-groundedness, agentic exploration, and cross-domain coverage. We introduce Agora, a benchmark pairing 362 questions with eight domain collections of 9,664 authentic documents and 372M tokens, far exceeding any model's context window, so agents must explore deliberately rather than scan exhaustively. Agora is built by an agentic pipeline combining cross-document task synthesis, leakage-preventing obfuscation, and difficulty filtering. Evaluating eight models, we find the task far from solved: even the strongest reaches only 59.4% accuracy, with notable variation across domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning Complementary Agent Groups via Task Hypergraphs for Next-POI Recommendation with Missing Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25801v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Next point-of-interest (POI) recommendation uses visit histories and POI content, but text and images are often missing. The challenge is to select agents that can jointly analyze the available evidence for each request. We propose \textsc{CATH} (\emph{Complementary Agents via Task Hypergraphs}), which learns to select agent groups for tasks such as comparing candidates or analyzing a POI with missing content. Agents with distinct processing roles are the task hypergraph nodes; each hyperedge connects the selected group and records its analysis goal and target POIs. The policy selects members conditioned on the request, task, and agents already selected. During execution, members share candidate-linked results that guide their subsequent operations. Completed tasks update candidate states and agent memories within the request. A shared ranking head converts candidate states into corrections to a fixed base ranking. Supervised ranking trains the executor, followed by policy optimization that accounts for computation cost. Experiments under natural missingness on Gowalla-CA, Foursquare-NYC, and Foursquare-TKY show 8.4--9.1\% relative NDCG@20 gains over HyperAgent4POI, the strongest prior baseline evaluated. | **[Safe, or Simply Incapable? Rethinking Safety Evaluation for Phone-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07630v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a phone-use agent avoids harm, does that show safety, or simply inability to act? Existing evaluations often cannot tell. A harmful outcome may be avoided because the agent recognized the risk and chose the safe action, or because it failed to understand the screen or execute any relevant action at all. These cases have different causes and call for different fixes, yet current benchmarks often merge them under task success, refusal, or final harmful outcome. We address this problem with PhoneSafety, a benchmark of 700 safety-critical moments drawn from real phone interactions across more than 130 apps. Each instance isolates the next decision at a risky moment and asks a simple question: does the model take the safe action, take the unsafe action, or fail to do anything useful? We evaluate eight representative phone-use agents under this framework. Our results reveal two main patterns. First, stronger general phone-use ability does not reliably imply safer choices at risky moments. Models that perform better on ordinary app tasks are not always the ones that behave more safely when the next action matters. Second, failures to do anything useful behave like a capability signal rather than a safety signal: they track broader phone-use performance and increase when visual input is removed. Across models, failures split into two recurring patterns: unsafe choices in settings where the model can act but chooses wrongly, and inability to act in more visually and operationally demanding screens. Overall, a harmless outcome is not enough to count as evidence of safety. Evaluating phone-use agents requires separating unsafe judgment from inability to act. Code and public data: \url{https://github.com/tangzhy/PhoneSafety}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[OpenSaC: A Python Library for Search as Code in Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25779v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research agents investigate complex questions through iterative search, source examination, and evidence synthesis. A conventional workflow alternates reasoning with individual search and reading actions. Search as Code introduces a new paradigm: agents write programs that compose search, source reading, reranking, model calls, and local processing, returning selected observations for further reasoning. To support this workflow in existing coding agents, we present OpenSaC, an open-source Python library with a companion Search as Code skill. Configurable capability interfaces let agents combine these operations while retaining evidence for inspection and reuse. Compared with standard Codex using its built-in web search, the OpenSaC configuration uses less execution time and fewer model tokens on BrowseComp and WideSearch, with higher accuracy on BrowseComp and slightly lower row F1 on WideSearch. We aim to lower the barrier to adopting Search as Code and provide an open foundation for community development and transparent research on deep research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Sourc...</summary><p>Source code: https://github.com/liuqi6777/OpenSaC</p></details> |
| **[Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Trace to Fix: Residual Task State in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25776v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering from failures in long-horizon, stateful language agents is challenging because the action introducing a defect, the symptom exposing it, and the persistent state requiring repair frequently diverge. Existing methods largely focus on retrospective failure attribution, leaving sequential diagnosis and state repair poorly integrated under constrained budgets. We introduce TRACE-FIX, a unified recovery framework centered on versioned residual task state. Trace transforms execution logs into a structured dependency-linked ledger, prioritizing unresolved task requirements rather than salient surface errors. Fix formulates recovery as budgeted planning over admissible actions, incorporating outcome forecasting and explicit repair-reserve constraints to prevent premature budget exhaustion. To rigorously decouple localization accuracy from downstream repair capability, we present LongH-Debug, evaluating root-cause attribution, verified-fault recovery, and end-to-end closed-loop execution independently. Our empirical analysis demonstrates that structured residual tracking substantially improves causal localization over raw-trace baselines, while budget-aware sequential planning converts verified fault signals into more reliable task recovery without destructive trial-and-error. Code is available at https://github.com/toffeecat/tracedp.</p></details> |  |
| **[Herculean: An Agentic Benchmark for Financial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14355v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As AI agents advance, the central question is no longer whether they can solve isolated financial tasks, but whether their capabilities generalize across complete professional workflows. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this question because they evaluate either static competencies or agents within a single interaction structure. We introduce Herculean, a benchmark of end-to-end agentic financial intelligence that crosses agent architecture, backbone model, and four structurally distinct workflows: Trading, Hedging, Market Insights, and Auditing. Each workflow is implemented as an MCP skill that preserves its native state, actions, constraints, and outcome criteria, allowing intact agent systems to be evaluated under matched external conditions. Across 30 architecture--backbone configurations, we find that agent capability is not globally ordered. Workflow rankings are weakly aligned, changing the architecture frequently reverses performance under the same backbone, and each architecture concentrates failure in different forms of workflow control. These results establish that agent performance is an interaction between architecture and workflow demand, rather than an intrinsic property of the underlying model. Consequently, success on a single financial workflow provides little evidence of dependable, general financial intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvalRx: Agentic Diagnosis and Verified Repair of Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25767v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Model evaluation reveals where models fail, but rarely why or whether a proposed fix genuinely works.
