EvalRx discovers systematic failure modes, explores candidate mechanisms, formulates falsifiable hypotheses, and verifies them by executing frozen, pre-specified tests before using supported diagnoses to guide tiered repair. Candidate repairs are validated against the unchanged model and promoted only when they improve the target behavior without unacceptable regression. Across language, vision-language, and audio-language settings spanning Gemini, Qwen, Gemma, and Nemotron, EvalRx improves performance in all 12 evaluated model family-modality combinations, with gains ranging from +1.4 to +10.9 percentage points (+5.8 points on average). These results show that model evaluation can serve not only to measure failures, but to support an auditable process for diagnosing and repairing them.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages. Technical report. Code and artifacts: https://github.com/evalvitals/evalrx</p></details> |

## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Distillation, Self-Training, and the Fragility of Learned Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24199v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating synthesis planning for small-molecule drug design requires chemical reasoners that are accurate as well as open to inspection and adaptation, yet frontier language models remain unreliable at predicting reaction outcomes and difficult to deploy, while small open-weight models lack the necessary domain reasoning. We show that frontier supervision can serve as a one-time bootstrap for open models on forward reaction prediction. Our models, Resonance, distill an open-weight teacher's reasoning traces and then learn from their own verified outputs, turning a small base model with near-zero accuracy into a capable reaction predictor, while a stronger student surpasses its teacher through distillation alone. Accuracy, however, does not bring robust reasoning: the small student inherits its teacher's single-pass style, self-training mainly sharpens answers it could already reach, and it readily abandons correct answers under pushback, suggesting that chemical reasoning models should be evaluated on the robustness of their reasoning as well as on the accuracy of their answers.</p></details> | 10 pages, 7 figures |
| **[On the Selectivity of Generative Models in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24124v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing small-molecule drugs that selectively bind to a target protein while avoiding unintended interactions with other proteins is essential for reducing side effects that contribute substantially to clinical attrition. Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/lovetaro/selectbench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a Presentation at the Neural Information Processing Systems 2026</p></details> |
| **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generative molecule evolution using 3D pharmacophore for efficient Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20130v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent breakthroughs in generative models have shown great promise for drug discovery, yet Structure-Based Drug Design (SBDD) faces two critical challenges: the scarcity of high-quality protein-ligand complex data and the practical synthesizability of generated molecules. While ligand-based approaches benefit from billion-scale small molecule datasets, they often lack the structural context of the target protein. To address these dual challenges of data scarcity and synthetic inaccessibility, we propose MEVO, a generative molecular evolutionary framework that operates within a synthesizable chemical space. MEVO introduces a reaction-aware generative model trained on reaction routes constructed by applying predefined reaction templates to Enamine Building Blocks. The model jointly generates molecules and corresponding synthesis sketches consistent with these templates. By integrating this generative capability into a pocket-aware evolutionary strategy, MEVO iteratively refines molecular structures within the synthesizable chemical space, guided by a hierarchical scoring function that combines fast approximation with rigorous Absolute Binding Free Energy Perturbation (ABFEP) calculations. This framework efficiently generates high-affinity, synthetically feasible binders for diverse protein targets. We validate MEVO's performance through rigorous ABFEP calculations, demonstrating its ability to design potent inhibitors for challenging targets such as KRAS$^{\textrm{G12D}}$, achieving affinities comparable to known active inhibitors. By integrating the data richness of ligand-based learning with the precision of structure-based optimization, MEVO offers a holistic solution that ensures both high binding affinity and synthetic accessibility, paving the way for more efficient and practical structure-based drug design.</p></details> |  |
| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. However, the relationship between design choices, training dynamics, and downstream generation quality remains poorly understood. We present a compute-controlled scaling study of CLMs comprising more than 30,000 experiments across molecular representations (SMILES, SELFIES, SAFE), tokenizations (atom-level and byte-pair encoding), model scales (0.5M-1B parameters), leakage-controlled datasets (MOSES, ChEMBL, PubChem, ZINC-22), and architectures (decoder-only and encoder-decoder). By fitting IsoFLOP profiles, we establish clear scaling trends in pretraining loss, but find that these improvements do not translate into comparable gains in goal-directed molecular design. Layer-wise probing and sparse autoencoder analysis reveal continued development of chemical representations: chemical syntax saturates early, while semantic properties emerge more slowly and become increasingly accessible with further training and model scale. Our approach balances hypothesis complexity and data fit through priors, which favour more general programs, and a likelihood, which favours accurate programs. Our experiments on several domains, including game playing and drug design, show that our method significantly outperforms previous methods, notably those that learn minimum description length programs. Our results also show that our approach is data-efficient and insensitive to example balance, including the ability to learn from exclusively positive examples.</p></details> |  |
| **[AutoBinder Agent: An MCP-Based Agent for End-to-End Protein Binder Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00019v1)** | 2026-01-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern AI technologies for drug discovery are distributed across heterogeneous platforms-including web applications, desktop environments, and code libraries-leading to fragmented workflows, inconsistent interfaces, and high integration overhead. | **[Multi-Constrained Evolutionary Molecular Design Framework: An Interpretable Drug Design Method Combining Rule-Based Evolution and Molecular Crossover](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10110v1)** | 2026-01-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study proposes MCEMOL (Multi-Constrained Evolutionary Molecular Design Framework), a molecular optimization approach integrating rule-based evolution with molecular crossover. MCEMOL employs dual-layer evolution: optimizing transformation rules at rule level while applying crossover and mutation to molecular structures. Unlike deep learning methods requiring large datasets and extensive training, our algorithm evolves efficiently from minimal starting molecules with low computational overhead. The framework incorporates message-passing neural networks and comprehensive chemical constraints, ensuring efficient and interpretable molecular design. Experimental results demonstrate that MCEMOL provides transparent design pathways through its evolutionary mechanism while generating valid, diverse, target-compliant molecules. Unlike black-box methods, MCEMOL delivers dual value: interpretable transformation rules researchers can understand and trust, alongside high-quality molecular libraries for practical applications. This establishes a paradigm where interpretable AI-driven drug design and effective molecular generation are achieved simultaneously, bridging the gap between computational innovation and practical drug discovery needs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EvoApplications 2026 (29th International Conference on the Applications of Evolutionary Computation), 15 pages, 4 figures. This version of the contribution has been accepted for publication, after peer review, but is not the Version of Record and does not reflect post-acceptance improvements, or any corrections. The Version of Record will be available online at Springer</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Affinity: A Benchmark of 1D, 2D, and 3D Methods Reveals Critical Trade-offs in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14283v1)** | 2026-01-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Currently, the field of structure-based drug design is dominated by three main types of algorithms: search-based algorithms, deep generative models, and reinforcement learning. We emphasize that 1D/2D ligand-centric drug design methods can be used in SBDD by treating the docking function as a black-box oracle, which is typically neglected. Our evaluation reveals distinct patterns across model categories. 3D structure-based models excel in binding affinities but show inconsistencies in chemical validity and pose quality. 1D models demonstrate reliable performance in standard molecular metrics but rarely achieve optimal binding affinities. 2D models offer balanced performance, maintaining high chemical validity while achieving moderate binding scores. Through detailed analysis across multiple protein targets, we identify key improvement areas for each model category, providing insights for researchers to combine strengths of different approaches while addressing their limitations. | **[Dynamics-inspired Structure Hallucination for Protein-protein Interaction Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06214v1)** | 2026-01-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-protein interaction (PPI) represents a central challenge within the biology field, and accurately predicting the consequences of mutations in this context is crucial for drug design and protein engineering. First, we introduce a structure refinement module trained by a mask mutation modeling (MMM) task on available wild-type structures, which is then transferred to produce the inaccessible mutant structures. | **[Exponential capacity scaling of classical GANs compared to hybrid latent style-based quantum GANs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05036v1)** | 2026-01-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantum generative modeling is a very active area of research in looking for practical advantage in data analysis. Quantum generative adversarial networks (QGANs) are leading candidates for quantum generative modeling and have been applied to diverse areas, from high-energy physics to image generation. The latent style-based QGAN, relying on a classical variational autoencoder to encode the input data into a latent space and then using a style-based QGAN for data generation has been proven to be efficient for image generation or drug design, hinting at the use of far less trainable parameters than their classical counterpart to achieve comparable performance, however this advantage has never been systematically studied. We present in this work the first comprehensive experimental analysis of this advantage of QGANS applied to SAT4 image generation, obtaining an exponential advantage in capacity scaling for a quantum generator in the hybrid latent style-based QGAN architecture. Careful tuning of the autoencoder is crucial to obtain stable, reliable results. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. | **[Beyond Chemical QA: Evaluating LLM's Chemical Reasoning with Modular Chemical Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21318v3)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large language models (LLMs) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning excel in mathematics and coding, their potential for systematic reasoning in chemistry, a domain demanding rigorous structural analysis for real-world tasks like drug design and reaction engineering, remains untapped. To address this, we introduce ChemCoTBench, a reasoning framework that bridges molecular structure understanding with arithmetic-inspired operations, including addition, deletion, and substitution, to formalize chemical problem-solving into transparent, step-by-step workflows. By treating molecular transformations as modular "chemical operations", the framework enables slow-thinking reasoning, mirroring the logic of mathematical proofs while grounding solutions in real-world chemical constraints. | **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. In this work, we introduce \textbf{ReACT-Drug}, a fully integrated, target-agnostic molecular design framework based on Reinforcement Learning. Unlike models requiring target-specific fine-tuning, ReACT-Drug utilizes a generalist approach by leveraging ESM-2 protein embeddings to identify similar proteins for a given target from a knowledge base such as Protein Data Base (PDB). Thereafter, the known drug ligands corresponding to such proteins are decomposed to initialize a fragment-based search space, biasing the agent towards biologically relevant subspaces. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Communication-Efficient Federated Domain Generalization in Edge Networks via Dynamic Prototype Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. However, deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) over wireless edge networks presents distinct challenges: large model parameters increase uplink transmission latency over bandwidth-constrained fading channels, and non-independent and identically distributed (Non-IID) data exacerbates client drift. Existing feature alignment solutions often introduce auxiliary computations that consume time slots allocated for wireless parameter transmission, increasing the likelihood of straggler dropouts under system delay constraints. To address these issues, we propose DPGR-GCAM, a communication-efficient federated domain generalization framework. By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. To mitigate client drift, we introduce the Gradient-Conflict Aware Modulation (GCAM) mechanism. GCAM operates within the low-dimensional feature manifold to dynamically resolve multi-objective optimization conflicts with minimal edge computation latency, thereby reducing computation-induced network outages. Experiments demonstrate that DPGR-GCAM improves cross-domain generalization and sustains convergence under network fading scenarios with a 10\% client participation rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Python Tool for Visualizing Variability of Pareto Fronts over Multiple Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08852v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperparameter optimization is crucial to achieving high performance in deep learning. On top of the performance, other criteria such as inference time or memory requirement often need to be optimized due to some practical reasons. This motivates research on multi-objective optimization (MOO). However, Pareto fronts of MOO methods are often shown without considering the variability caused by random seeds and this makes the performance stability evaluation difficult. Although there is a concept named \emph{empirical attainment surface} to enable the visualization with uncertainty over multiple runs, there is no major Python package for empirical attainment surface. We, therefore, develop a Python package for this purpose and describe the usage. The package is available at https://github.com/nabenabe0928/empirical-attainment-func. OptunaHub now provides our implementation at https://hub.optuna.org/visualization/plot_empirical_attainment_surface/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to AutoML workshop</p></details> |
| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. In practice, however, stakeholders must translate their qualitative preferences between different criteria into numerical weights, priorities, or reference points required by optimization methods, which can be difficult to specify and interpret. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. We then present a simulation study using an LLM-based DM agent and benchmark ParetoChat against interactive methods implemented in DESDEO, including reference-point and NIMBUS methods. The computational experiments on multi-objective binary knapsack and set-partitioning instances show that ParetoChat can guide the search efficiently toward solutions that match the DM's preferences. An application to historical courier-order assignment instances illustrates how users can explore objective extremes and intermediate trade-offs through natural-language requests. The results indicate that LLM agents may provide a practical interaction layer between business stakeholders and optimization tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. When renovation is delayed beyond the infrastructure's remaining lifespan, costly emergency interventions become necessary to prevent failure. Decision makers must therefore balance expected emergency intervention costs against traffic congestion impacts. We formalize this trade-off as a road network maintenance scheduling problem with uncertain deadlines, which presents optimization challenges including computationally expensive evaluation and an exponentially growing solution space. We formulate the problem as a bi-level multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly accounts for uncertain infrastructure lifespans through probabilistic failure models. We develop a progressive lower bound evaluation method: candidate schedules receive quick, optimistic estimates of their traffic impact from machine learning based surrogate models, and these estimates are refined by exact traffic simulation only for promising candidates, so that full evaluation is reserved almost exclusively for schedules that can advance the Pareto front. Embedded in a multi-objective genetic algorithm, this evaluation strategy improves solution quality by enabling many more iterations within a fixed computational budget. We demonstrate the method's effectiveness and scalability on substantially larger problem instances than previously addressed in the literature: 76 and 80 projects on the Sioux Falls and Anaheim networks, respectively. Under an identical 24-hour computational budget across 30 random seeds, the method completes up to 87 times as many optimization iterations as standard NSGA-II and achieves statistically significant improvements across multiple performance metrics over both standard NSGA-II and a surrogate-assisted baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures. Accepted manuscript of the article published in Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cacaie.2026.100174</p></details> |
| **[In-Context Preference-Conditioned Pareto Set Learning from Limited Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21736v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization involves diverse preferences over conflicting objectives. Rather than returning a finite set of trade-off solutions, preference-conditioned Pareto set learning seeks to map a specified preference directly to a corresponding Pareto optimal solution. Yet in many real-world scenarios, optimization must proceed offline without further objective evaluations, leaving only a limited static dataset that sparsely covers the decision and objective spaces. Existing methods either optimize separately for each preference or train a problem-specific Pareto solution model, requiring repeated search across preferences or repeated training across problems. We introduce in-context preference-conditioned Pareto set learning (ICPSL), a framework that amortizes both preference-wise solution search and problem-specific model construction. ICPSL pretrains a foundation-style hypernetwork on a large and diverse set of synthetic prior data. Given the offline observations of an unseen problem, the pretrained hypernetwork learns to generate a problem-specific, preference-conditioned Pareto solution model in a single forward pass. The generated model can then be reused to predict solutions for arbitrary preferences without problem-specific training or preference-wise search. Experiments on synthetic and real-world problems show that ICPSL produces high-quality Pareto solutions while providing substantial speedups in problem-specific solution-model construction.</p></details> |  |
| **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures, Conference: MLCAD '26: 8th ACM/IEEE International Symposium on Machine Learning for CAD, September 7 to 9, 2026, Jeju, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[First Mathematical Runtime Analyses of Evolutionary Multitask Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21311v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To facilitate the solving of complex problems, evolutionary multitask optimization is regarded as a promising technique due to its capacity for cross-task knowledge transfer. In contrast to empirical evidences, no mathematical runtime support exists for this technique within the domain of multi-objective optimization. In this work, we aim to fill this important gap. We analyze how the representative multiobjective multifactorial evolutionary algorithm (MO-MFEA) covers the Pareto fronts of two bi-objective problems simultaneously. For the two-task problem of maximizing the classic bi-objective OMM and the multimodal OJZJ benchmarks, the MO-MFEA requires only an expected runtime of $O(n^2 \log n)$---the same as optimizing the simple OMM alone---yet achieves a speedup by a factor of $Θ(n^{k-1}/\log n)$ compared to optimizing OJZJ alone (with gap size $k$). Similarly, for the task pair comprising the classic LOTZ and the multimodal DLTB benchmarks, a speedup of $Θ(n)$ is attained over optimizing DLTB alone, while the runtime matches that of optimizing LOTZ alone.</p></details> |  |
| **[When the implementation matters more than the algorithm: NSGA-II and other multi-objective optimizers in engineering design tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22397v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering design tools increasingly embed optimizers as ready-made components that users select by algorithm name, while operator settings and bound, integer and duplicate handling stay fixed inside each tool. Eighteen configurations were run at 5,000 evaluations under three population allocations, with statistics fixed in advance. Four NSGA-II implementations differed by up to 5% in hypervolume, more than three canonical algorithms of different families differed from one another, and their response to population size differed by up to 10 percentage points; for two MOEA/D implementations, by 28. On ZDT test functions the same NSGA-II implementations differed up to 470-fold in IGD. An ablation in one open-source NSGA-II reproduced the behaviour of one tool from its documented settings but not the gaps of the other two, so documented parameters do not identify an implementation. Counting the budget in wall-clock time reversed the ranking of the model-based optimizer, and reporting the final population instead of the archive understated some configurations by up to a third; most differences fell below the surrogate's error. For optimizers embedded in design-support systems, the implementation is part of the method; a reporting checklist is derived: implementation and version, effective settings, budget in time and evaluations, and archive-based indicators.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables. Per-run data, emulator models and analysis scripts: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23157761</p></details> |
| **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. Contemporary methods mitigate this challenge by exploiting pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for their learned transferable features. Some recent ones augment the scarce data by retrieving relevant yet out-of-distribution (OOD) examples from external data sources. However, they largely overlook an equally fundamental challenge: the absence of validation data for model selection and hyperparameter tuning. As a result, the adapted VLMs overfit the few-shot training data and generalize poorly to OOD test data. We show that retrieved data provide a natural source of validation, but their direct use creates a fundamental paradox. Since the retrieved samples are inherently OOD w.r.t the few-shot ID training data, adapting a VLM to the ID data constantly degrades its performance on the retrieved data. Consequently, conventional validation relying on the accuracy of retrieved data favors no adaptation and prevents improved generalization. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. This strategy enables hyperparameter tuning, which had been unrealistically done by using test data, thereby mitigating overfitting and improving generalization. We integrate it into a stage-wise finetuning pipeline, termed Validation-Enabled Stage-wise Tuning (VEST). Extensive experiments on five established benchmarks show that VEST consistently outperforms existing approaches on both ID and OOD test data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://hannawang09.github.io/projects/vest/</p></details> |
| **[Large Language Models for Model-Based Robot Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33423v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can contribute useful engineering knowledge to robot design, but directly generated designs may rely on implicit assumptions and provide no guarantees of feasibility or optimality. These assumptions are critical because different reasonable modeling choices can materially change which designs are predicted to be feasible or optimal. We therefore present a framework that uses LLMs to construct explicit engineering models containing physical relationships, compatibility constraints, and objectives, allowing these modeling choices to be inspected and revised before formal optimization. The model can then be updated with additional engineering, manufacturer, or system-specific information before formal multi-objective optimization provides feasibility and Pareto-optimality guarantees with respect to the finalized model and specified design space. Comparisons with an independently developed expert model and successive stages of model refinement further showed that changes in modeling assumptions substantially altered the predicted feasible and Pareto-optimal design sets. Together, these results show that using LLMs to construct explicit engineering models makes the underlying design choices available for inspection and revision before those assumptions determine the optimized designs. Explicit modeling therefore provides an interface for combining LLM-generated engineering knowledge, system-specific information, and formal design optimization.</p></details> | 9 pages, 4 figures |
| **[When Rewards Disagree: Reward Weighting for Token-Efficient Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04415v4)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human perception interprets video dynamics by constructing structured mental representations of entities, actions, and temporal boundaries before engaging in high-level reasoning. To address this, we introduce Structured Event Evidence, a unified, temporally ordered schema designed for general video reasoning. However, post-training a model to extract structured evidence while reasoning introduces a severe multi-objective optimization bottleneck, as candidate rollouts must simultaneously satisfy schema compliance, task accuracy, and reasoning budget. Standard reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms attempt to balance these objectives via static scalarization, enforcing a rigid accuracy-length trade-off across training and obscuring fine-grained reward signals. We resolve this by proposing Group-Relative Advantage Balancing (GRAB), which dynamically computes Pareto-optimal objective weights within each rollout group via a minimum-norm criterion over centered rewards. Operating strictly in reward space, GRAB incurs virtually zero computational overhead (<0.002% relative runtime to GRPO) and eliminates brittle weight tuning. Across temporal grounding and video question-answering benchmarks, GRAB generates 13% to 39% fewer tokens while maintaining competitive accuracy with exhaustively hand-tuned static weights -- without requiring hyperparameter weight sweeps.</p></details> |  |
| **[Harmony Harness: Multi-Objective Self-Evolution of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15150v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work on self-evolving LLM agents treats the agent harness as an optimization target that can be iteratively improved through execution feedback. | **[SURF: Steering the Scalarization Weight to Uniformly Traverse the Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20619v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalarization is widely used in multi-objective optimization owing to its simplicity and scalability. In many applications, the goal is to generate solutions that represent diverse user preferences, ideally with uniform coverage of the Pareto front (PF). As the weight varies, the corresponding solutions traverse the PF at a generally nonuniform speed. Integrating this speed gives cumulative arc length. After normalization by the total length, it defines a cumulative distribution function (CDF) on weights whose inverse selects points at equal arc-length intervals. Building on this insight, we propose SURF (Sampling Uniformly along the Pareto Front). For structured problems, including bi-objective bandits, we derive analytical expressions for this CDF map and the resulting PF-aware weight sampling rule. For general problems, SURF alternates between CDF reconstruction and weight sampling. | **[Scheduling in Cloud,Fog, and Edge Computing: A Comparative Analysis and Decision Framework for Technique Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13830v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing heterogeneity and dynamism of cloud, fog, and edge computing make scheduling a challenging problem involving diverse resource capabilities, workload characteristics, latency and energy constraints, and competing optimization objectives. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems. Furthermore, we formulate a multi-objective optimization problem and introduce a first-order efficient Pareto optimizer that jointly optimizes LLM parameters and the soft prompt, rigorously enforcing orthogonality between new task updates and the estimated pre-training gradients. Existing FHE Transformers use hand-tuned approximation settings, such as iteration count and polynomial degree, applied uniformly across layers, models, and tasks. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. The problem is hard: the decision space is large (96 or 256 variables), each configuration takes 70 to 1,000 seconds to evaluate even in cleartext, and 85 to 90 percent of configurations are invalid. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Representing Information on DNA using Patterns Induced by Enzymatic Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24171v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enzymatic DNA labeling is a powerful tool with applications in biochemistry, molecular biology, biotechnology, medical science, and genomic research. This paper contributes to the evolving field of DNA-based data storage by presenting a formal framework for modeling DNA labeling in strings, specifically tailored for data storage purposes. Our approach involves a known DNA molecule as a template for labeling, employing patterns induced by a set of designed labels to represent information. One hypothetical implementation can use CRISPR-Cas9 and gRNA reagents for labeling. Various aspects of the general labeling channel, including fixed-length labels, are explored, and upper bounds on the maximum size of the corresponding codes are given. The study includes the development of an efficient encoder-decoder pair that is proven to be optimal in terms of maximum code size under specific conditions. The codewords for this framework are codesets, i.e., each codeword is a collection of a few labels. Of special interest are codes whose codesets contain fixed-length labels. Their construction and analysis are tied to de Bruijn sequences and constrained coding.</p></details> |  |
| **[Surveying Experimentally-Validated Components for DNA-Based Molecular Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23361v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular communication is a novel paradigm for the design of nanonetworks. It uses molecules as information carriers and enables communication between nanodevices. DNA-based molecular communication is a promising approach for medical applications, such as in-body networks for diagnostics and drug delivery. However, the transition from theoretical models to practical experiments is still in its early stages. This survey reviews experimentally validated DNA-tiles, nanocarriers, and biosensors relevant to implementing DNA-tile-based nanonetworks in vitro. We evaluate these components based on their stability, compatibility, cost-effectiveness, and overall suitability for wet-lab implementation. Based on this analysis, we identify promising component combinations and derive a blueprint for a minimal proof-of-concept experiment. The resulting framework provides researchers with both a structured overview of experimentally relevant design choices and a concrete starting point for implementing and validating DNA-based nanonetworks in the wet lab.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 7 Tables, 8 Figures, under submission for IEEE COMST</p></details> |

For codes with no mixed columns, this yields the hyperbolic constraint $s_1/E_1 + s_2/E_2 \le 1$, which we conjecture to hold universally whenever $\max\{s_1,s_2\} \ge 2$. | **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. In practice, whether exposing such a quasi-evidence group violates privacy also depends on sender-specified disclosure policies. We introduce QuasiLeak-Bench, a benchmark for policy-conditioned quasi-evidence group recovery, which evaluates whether detectors recover complete quasi-evidence groups and flag the disclosures the given policy prohibits. To obtain reliable ground truth at scale, a reverse-construction pipeline first selects weak quasi-identifiers, composes them into quasi-evidence groups verified to jointly reveal a target attribute, and generates documents around this evidence, followed by automated quality control. Experiments show that existing privacy detectors transfer poorly to QuasiLeak-Bench, and locally deployable LLM-based detectors fall far behind proprietary models, mainly because they miss most quasi-evidence groups altogether. However, a 4B model fine-tuned on our training split surpasses the strongest prompted model on held-out documents, showing that the task is learnable.</p></details> | <details><summary>37 pa...</summary><p>37 pages, 15 figures, 25 tables, v2: Figure 1 updated</p></details> |
| **[UZHCL_for_the_win at Touche: Encoder Fine-Tuning, Synthetic Augmentation, and an Underpowered Encoder-LLM Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17077v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online discussions on platforms like Reddit are a productive testbed for fallacy detection: short texts, broad topical range, and the full inventory of informal fallacies. Our submitted systems are fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoders with task-specific heads, augmented with a small batch of LLM-generated synthetic contrastive pairs; for ST2 and ST3 on the enhanced track we additionally added confidence-filtered pseudo-labelled examples from the unlabelled pool. We also characterise how much of the enhanced track's lift reflects organiser-rewritten inputs carrying label information rather than genuine argument clarification. On the official Touche 2026 test set our systems ranked first on both base-track classification cells (ST2 and ST3) and second on both binary-detection (ST1) tracks, with the two enhanced-track classification cells topped by cascaded and multitask systems. Code: https://github.com/psychias/fallacy_detection_clef_2026</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages. Working notes for Touche at CLEF 2026 (Jena, Germany)</p></details> |
| **[Test-Time Augmentation for LLMs: Where to Inject Diversity at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09351v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aggregating several LLM responses to the same question can improve accuracy, but every method that does so must decide where the responses should differ. Meaning-preserving paraphrases of the input yield the largest accuracy gains (+1.80 pp over a single call) and reach peak accuracy with the fewest samples. Yet, they are not the most diverse: character-level noise makes the responses disagree far more often (37.8% versus 27.4%) while gaining less accuracy, and perturbing two modalities at once falls below the single-call baseline. Not all diversity is equally useful: perturbations that keep the meaning intact produce disagreement that aggregation can use, while those that corrupt it produce disagreement that is wrong. Overall, our results suggest that for mid-tier LLMs such as Claude Haiku, varying how a question is phrased is a better use of an inference call than varying the reasoning path. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Recent studies have developed LLM-based methods to address this problem. However, the executability and correctness of the generated plans remain suboptimal in complex environments and tasks. On the other hand, classical search algorithms have the potential to solve the planning problems perfectly, yet they require fully observable environments, known state transitions and goal state, which are often unattainable in general task domains. Therefore, we propose to implement an LLM-guided search algorithm that makes the search process possible and efficient by incorporating LLMs' commonsense knowledge. Our method leverages LLMs to (1) translate natural language instructions into formatted goal conditions, (2) facilitate efficient exploration in partially observable environments, (3) implement a rule-based world simulator, and (4) prune the space of candidate actions to improve search efficiency. Experimental results on VirtualHome and ALFWorld demonstrate its superior performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026 findings. Code will be released at https://github.com/woyut/TESS_EMNLP26</p></details> |
| **[Budgeted Attack Scheduling for LLM Red-Teaming: The Roles of Static Priors, Semantic Guidance, and Online Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25897v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) become increasingly diverse, red-teaming has come to rely on large libraries of jailbreak prompts and attack strategies to evaluate the robustness of victim models and their defense settings. However, attack effectiveness is highly condition-dependent: an attack that succeeds under one victim-defense setting may fail under another, while exhaustively evaluating the full attack library for each setting incurs substantial token and monetary costs. This motivates studying how jailbreak attacks should be prioritized under a limited attempt budget for efficient robustness evaluation. In this work, we formalize this problem as budgeted attack scheduling over a fixed attack library and investigate three complementary signals under a fixed victim-defense condition: (1) static historical attack success rate (ASR) priors; (2) semantic guidance from LLMs; and (3) online feedback from earlier queries. Building on these signals, we design four scheduling strategies and evaluate them across three victim models and three defense settings on GPT-5.2. Extensive experiments clearly characterize the conditions under which each signal is most effective. Historical ASR provides a strong initialization, but its reliability degrades under defense-induced distribution shifts. Semantic guidance consistently improves or matches static prior-based scheduling in the cold-start regime, while online feedback becomes particularly valuable when transferred priors become stale. Semantic guidance and online feedback can also partially compensate for missing historical priors. Taken together, these findings characterize budgeted red-teaming as an attack scheduling problem in which historical priors, semantic guidance, and online feedback play complementary roles.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Orchestrated within a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm, ACTS introduces a novel dual-attention mechanism. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. | **[AURA-Eval: Evaluation Framework for Acting Under Risk Awareness in LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06783v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly taking actions with real consequences, yet safety evaluations often reduce their behavior to a final safe/unsafe outcome, obscuring whether the agent recognized the risk and how it responded. It also creates paired scenarios that preserve the underlying goal but differ in whether safe fulfillment is available, allowing unsafe compliance and over-refusal to be evaluated together. An LLM-judge panel, validated against human annotations, evaluates each continuation along three axes: risk detection from the reasoning trace, and action type and safety from the action. Across all 20 models, unsafe-action rates are higher when no safe path for request fulfillment is available than when one is; for frontier models, the rates are 27-40% versus 5-7%. When a safe path exists, frontier models rarely refuse; when none exists, they most often propose an alternative. Open-weight models, by contrast, more often execute the request than propose alternatives and act unsafely on 58-83% of items. | **[When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. These neurons cluster in the query and value projections of later layers and exhibit highly asymmetric cross-category transferability. Furthermore, targeted fine-tuning of these neurons substantially improves fairness while preserving general capabilities. Importantly, models tuned with I-CAN-Debias generalize robustly across diverse evaluations beyond the training distribution, spanning open-ended fairness, jailbreak attacks, and implicit bias, without requiring test-time debiasing prompts. | **[Service-Induced Congestion in Memory-Constrained LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15555v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In large language model (LLM) serving, each request accumulates persistent graphics processing unit (GPU) memory during service as its key-value cache grows with every generated token. Under high concurrency, aggregate memory usage therefore increases endogenously over time: the service process itself creates future capacity pressure. When memory capacity is exceeded, systems evict active requests, discarding cached state and restarting them later, which wastes computation and reduces throughput. In the saturated-input regime, the system admits both eviction-free fixed points and limit cycles with evictions. For homogeneous workloads, we show that the eviction-free equilibrium is unstable and that, except for a Lebesgue-measure-zero exact-capture set, the system converges to a unique worst-case limit cycle that is asymptotically stable outside this exceptional set, with throughput losses as large as 50%. For heterogeneous workloads, we prove a stability criterion in the two-class common-input setting and explain how the survival-polynomial mechanism generalizes to multiple classes and heterogeneous-input lengths. Under an input-dominated scaling regime, coprime decoding lengths stabilize the eviction-free equilibrium, while non-coprime lengths create synchronized modes that drive instability. These results characterize when workload heterogeneity desynchronizes completions and helps stabilize memory-constrained serving. More broadly, we identify service-induced congestion as a structural instability mechanism and derive scheduling design principles for sustaining high throughput.</p></details> | <details><summary>100 p...</summary><p>100 pages. v2: adds GPU experiments with benchmark workloads (Appendix D.4) and revises the figures</p></details> |
| **[How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, including appendices</p></details> |

At 50\% target routing sparsity, WIDE improves Llama3.1-8B average zero-shot accuracy by up to 20.26 points over the strongest dynamic-depth baseline under calibration-only settings, while retaining 97.6\%/88.2\% of dense Qwen3-14B likelihood/generation performance after LoRA tuning. | **[RouteWeaver: Weaving Mode Selection and Execution into Unified LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19705v3)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM routing dynamically selects and coordinates heterogeneous language models across diverse tasks. To jointly optimize mode selection and within-mode execution, we develop COMET-GRPO (COordinated Mode-Execution Training), which decouples the trajectory-level advantage into separate signals for mode selection and within-mode execution, enabling more targeted credit assignment to each decision level. We further introduce progressive exploration, which gradually shifts training from externally assigned modes to autonomous selection, so that the router first learns how to execute each mode before deciding when to use it. We evaluate RouteWeaver across mathematics, coding, knowledge, logical reasoning, and factual recall. RouteWeaver achieves an average accuracy of 61.4%, an 11.03% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, and outperforms all compared baselines in each of the five domains. | **[SoK: Systematizing LLM Prompt Security: Taxonomies, Datasets, and Unified Evaluation of Attacks and Defenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.15476v5)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prompt-security claims for large language models (LLMs) are often compared as properties of individual attacks or defenses, although measured harmfulness depends on the target model, access setting, attack and defense configuration, prompt sample, and judger. Lower attack success can reflect failure to execute transformed requests rather than stronger safety; attack-category advantages can reverse when one method is excluded, and attack success varies with assistant choice. | **[DebugLM: Learning Traceable Training Data Provenance for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17884v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are trained through multi-stage pipelines over heterogeneous data sources, yet developers lack a principled way to pinpoint the specific data responsible for an observed behavior. These findings show how hidden label shortcuts can masquerade as scientific reasoning or discovery, motivating source-level audits before benchmark gains are trusted.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages. | **[ATEST: Adaptive Resource-Aware Test-Time Scaling for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25519v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by sampling multiple answers, but fixed sampling budgets ignore both query difficulty and the changing energy cost of inference under GPU contention. | **[Figures as Interfaces: Toward LLM-Native Artifacts for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08491v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are transforming scientific workflows, not only through their generative capabilities but also through their emerging ability to use tools, reason about data, and coordinate complex analytical tasks. In this paper, we introduce the concept of LLM-native figures: data-driven artifacts that are simultaneously human-legible and machine-addressable. Unlike traditional plots, each artifact embeds complete provenance: the data subset, analytical operations and code, and visualization specification used to generate it. As a result, an LLM can "see through" the figure--tracing selections back to their sources, generating code to extend analyses, and orchestrating new visualizations through natural-language instructions or direct manipulation. We implement this concept through a hybrid language-visual interface that integrates LLM agents with a bidirectional mapping between figures and underlying data. Using the science of science domain as a testbed, we demonstrate that LLM-native figures can accelerate discovery, improve reproducibility, and make reasoning transparent across agents and users. More broadly, this work establishes a general framework for embedding provenance, interactivity, and explainability into the artifacts of modern research, redefining the figure not as an end product, but as an interface for discovery. | **[Where Are Language-Specific Features Represented in Multilingual LLMs? | **[Consistent LLM Call Handling Across Java Frameworks: A Reference Implementation and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25480v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Java applications reach large language models (LLMs) through an integration framework, and rely on it to handle a failing provider: to retry what can be retried and nothing else, to wait as long as the provider asks, never to present a truncated answer as complete, and to record each failure in the application's metrics. LangChain4j meets it in 47 of 77 applicable cases with its defaults and in 51 of 83 when configured explicitly, Spring AI in 72 of 77 and 76 of 83; both deliver a stream that ended without its terminator as a complete answer. We then place one implementation of the contract beneath both frameworks, at the HTTP boundary - an OkHttp interceptor for Spring AI, an HttpClient for LangChain4j, and a java.net.http transport for code without a framework - sharing a 725-line core that depends only on the JDK and the Micrometer Observation API. With it, all three clients behave identically and meet the contract in 90 of 91 cases; the remaining case is a policy trade-off between retrying a hung provider and waiting for a slow first token. Scoring options on copies of the prompt cache matches full-sequence scoring to within 1.4e-6 in probability. Adding dialogue, industry and complaint data (System X+) improves support-task results but lowers IFEval accuracy from 55.5% to 45.0%. Against its base LLM, JevBench accuracy rises from 55.0% to 61.0% (p=0.04), with no statistically significant IFEval change (p=0.71). A matched latency test on unadapted Qwen3-1.7B saves 17-39%. | **[MOSAIC: Multi-Objective Search for Allocating Inference Cache Across LLM Decoder Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25431v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Key-Value (KV) cache is the main memory bottleneck of long-context LLM inference. Against EvolKV, MOSAIC explores a ~10^40 x larger search space with 4x fewer evaluations and 18% fewer GPU-hours per budget, and scores higher in 5 of 6 settings. Rather than hand-crafting personas, we employ an evolutionary coding agent to discover persona generation programs optimized for human-likeness and behavioral coverage over real user conversations. The evolved program generates diverse, human-like personas for any task in the domain. LoGIST maps each input instance to a decision diagram, a directed acyclic graph that captures the underlying logical structure, and encodes this graph into a logical embedding that conditions the LLM's reasoning process. | **[VReAct: Verification as an Architectural Layer for LLM Agents: A V-Model Design, and a Pilot Study of Its Deterministic Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31937v3)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents built on the ReAct pattern concentrate four responsibilities in one model: selecting a strategy, choosing each action, formatting it, and judging whether the result is adequate. We propose VReAct, which treats verification as an architectural layer for ReAct agents by adapting the software-engineering V-model: specification levels descend from requirements to individual steps, each paired with a dedicated verifier; a deterministic controller enforces every verdict; and only verification outcomes write memory. A rejection thus localizes the level that introduced the fault, and an agent halts by declining rather than by exhaustion. | **[Complementary Vulnerabilities: Auditing and Governing Clinician-LLM Error Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25279v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Health systems are deploying large language models (LLMs) into clinical workflows, often on the assumption that a second automated check can only reduce error. The two reasoners' failure substrates differ: they are embodied and cognitive in the clinician, and statistical and generative in the model - so their weak points need not line up. That divergence is plausible rather than guaranteed, and the independence it creates is fragile, eroding when clinicians defer to the model or the model conforms to the clinician. We therefore propose a predeployment audit in which each site measures error overlap on its own cases. We also propose a governance model with which the site keeps those errors independent over time.
