| **[It's Complicated: Factors Influencing the Effectiveness of LLM-Generated Feedback in a Live Programming Course](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25244v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We report on three iterations of an LLM-generated feedback system deployed in a live web development course, spanning three underlying models and three feedback formats. We ask how feedback presence and its characteristics (length, complexity, format) relate to student performance, whether prior feedback exposure is associated with final-project performance, and how performance changes after first exposure. Feedback was associated with more favorable immediate outcomes in several comparisons, but the pattern varied by outcome and period. In pooled models, longer feedback was associated with lower next-submission correctness, more remaining attempts, and longer resubmission time. Prior same-part feedback was associated with lower final-project solve probability. Student-level comparisons found differences on final-project steps begun before exposure, while within-student changes across steps were uncertain. These comparisons do not identify the causal effect of feedback. Changes on standard exercises were more favorable among exposed students, but the groups' different course-stage splits limit causal interpretation. Bounded message length and student choice of format are directions for experimental evaluation. Our final-project analysis captures follow-up performance rather than directly measuring learning or understanding.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentLens: Framework-Aware Characterization and Resource Control for Heterogeneous LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25231v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed on shared hosts, yet their host-side resource demands remain poorly understood, limiting effective resource coordination. We present AgentLens, which combines a cross-framework workload characterization with a framework-aware resource controller for co-located agents. Across 10 frameworks and 11 matched coding tasks, we find that framework orchestration strongly shapes resource demand: framework identity explains 52.95% of peak RSS variance, compared with 4.22% for task identity, while execve events per tool call differ by 39x between MetaGPT and CAMEL. Resource dimensions are not interchangeable: peak RSS and subprocess count have a Spearman correlation of 0.13. Static budgets and framework-agnostic policies miss these structural differences and the alternation between API waiting and tool execution. AgentLens combines offline framework fingerprints with online execution-phase signals to coordinate memory through cgroup v2, without modifying framework internals. In trace-driven replay across three co-location pairs at a memory budget of 90% of aggregate peak demand, AgentLens keeps the protected worker's P95 allocation latency at 5.7-7.1 ms, improving on static allocation by 2.1-2.6x.</p></details> |  |
| **[gKSM: GPU Memory Deduplication at Driver Layer for Serverless LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25219v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In serverless LLM inference, cloud providers commonly locate multiple instances on a single GPU to improve utilization. However, each instance independently maintains its model weights and runtime state, making duplicate GPU memory the primary bottleneck. Memory deduplication is a widely-used technique for eliminating duplicate data in CPU systems, but extending it to GPU memory faces fundamental challenges. Effective GPU memory deduplication must avoid redundant data movement between CPU and GPU while preserving write isolation for shared pages without native copy-on-write (COW) support. This paper presents gKSM, a transparent GPU memory deduplication framework for serverless LLM inference. gKSM addresses these challenges through CPU-GPU collaborative hash management that offloads hash computation to GPU kernels, zero-copy model weight deduplication that eliminates duplication before model weight transfer, and a cross-privilege page fault handler that combines a lightweight kernel hook and CUDA Virtual Memory Management (VMM) remapping in the user space. gKSM also designs a stable page scanning algorithm and a reuse-aware placement policy in cluster scale. Experiments show that gKSM reduces GPU memory usage by 59.8% while maintaining high throughput compared to ServerlessLLM. Under memory constrained workloads, gKSM improves aggregate throughput by 2.15X and supports five instances concurrently. In cluster level simulation, gKSM achieves a service rate of 99.97%, compared to 78.2% for SLInfer and 26.9% for ServerlessLLM.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by SIGOPS ATC 2026</p></details> |
| **[BrandEval: A Two-Track Multi-Agent Benchmark for Risk-Sensitive LLM Crisis Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25208v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating LLMs for crisis communication requires more than judging isolated responses, because public feedback and competitor actions can reshape trust over time. Existing benchmarks remain largely static and single-turn, offering limited evidence about long-horizon risk-sensitive communication. We introduce \textbf{BrandEval}, a two-track benchmark for product-crisis communication, focusing on public brand responses to product-related safety, quality, service, and trust incidents. BrandEval-Static evaluates stakeholder-aware strategic analysis, while BrandEval-Dynamic stress-tests response policies in \textbf{BrandPolis}, a controlled multi-agent sandbox with consumer feedback, competitor responses, and trust-risk trajectories. We also construct \textbf{BrandSRD}, a Chinese crisis-response dataset, and instantiate a reference Strategic Agent using \textbf{Strategic Rationale (SR)}. Across proprietary and open-source models, we find that static strategic quality is informative but incomplete: it aligns with some end-state outcomes but does not reliably predict volatility and delayed recovery; dynamic stress tests further expose tail-risk failures under manipulative or weakly grounded policies. The code and dataset will be available at https://github.com/htgan-ai/BrandEval.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP2026 Findings</p></details> |
| **[RuPLaR : Efficient Latent Compression of LLM Reasoning Chains with Rule-Based Priors From Multi-Step to One-Step](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09346v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Chain-of-Thought (CoT) paradigm, while enhancing the interpretability of Large Language Models (LLMs), is constrained by the inefficiencies and expressive limits of natural language. Latent Chain-of-Thought (latent CoT) reasoning, which operates in a continuous latent space, offers a promising alternative but faces challenges from structural complexities in existing multi-step or multi-model paradigms, such as error propagation and coordination overhead. In this paper, we introduce One-Model One-Step, a novel compression framework for Latent Reasoning with Rule-Based Priors(RuPLaR) to address this challenge. Our method trains an LLM to autonomously generate latent reasoning tokens in a single training stage, guided by rule-based prior probability distributions, thereby eliminating cascaded processes and inter-model dependencies. To ensure reasoning quality, we design a joint training objective that enforces answer consistency via cross-entropy, aligns soft token distributions with rule-based priors via KL divergence (the Soft Thinking constraint), and adds a problem-thought semantic alignment constraint in the representation space. Extensive experiments show that our compression framework not only improves accuracy by 11.03% over existing latent CoT methods on GSM8k-Aug but also achieves this with minimal token usage, underscoring its effectiveness and extensibility. Code: https://github.com/xiaocen-luo/RuPLaR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Uber-Join: Unifying Diverse Table Joins using LLMs with Precision and Cost Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25178v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When two tables from different sources are joined, the values that should join often differ in spelling, format, or meaning, and fuzzy-, transformation-, and semantic-joins each handle one type of these differences. Large language models (LLMs) can match values that differ in several ways at once, but every LLM call is billed, and having an LLM compare the rows of two large tables can cost far more than a typical budget allows. This paper studies how to combine these join types with LLMs to join two tables under a dollar budget, maximizing recall while keeping the precision of the result at or above a target, a problem that is NP-hard. Our key idea is to use LLMs selectively to create join programs and verify their outputs through sampling, while adaptively deciding which joins to pursue under budget and precision constraints. This combines the scalability of program-based joins with selective LLM verification, while maintaining a statistical precision guarantee on the accumulated result. We develop Uber-Join, which treats creating a join and checking the output of a created join as two levels of arms in a budgeted multi-armed bandit. It commits an output only if the precision of the whole result, estimated from an LLM's judgments of sampled pairs, stays at or above the target with high confidence. Under the same budget, Uber-Join achieves the highest recall at the precision target in nearly all settings on three join benchmarks and Join-Hard, a new benchmark of large tables.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do LLMs Reproduce the Age Gap in Loneliness? Evaluating Survey Simulation against European Survey Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25158v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are often used to simulate survey respondents, but matching survey averages does not establish accurate demographic differences. We test whether LLM outputs reproduce within-country age gaps in loneliness using the 2022 EU Loneliness Survey, comparing adults aged 18-34 and 65 or older in France, Italy, Poland, and Sweden. We evaluate simulation with and without age information and direct distribution estimation against a probability-based sample, with a non-probability sensitivity reference. Both human samples show higher mean loneliness among younger adults. Under age-specified simulation with standard response-option order, Mistral Small 4 and GPT-5.6 Terra reverse this ordering under all three prompt framings in three and two countries, respectively, mainly by overestimating older-adult means. DeepSeek V4 Flash preserves the ordering everywhere but underestimates both group means, with errors largely cancelling in France and Italy. Against the probability-based reference, direct estimation has lower absolute age-gap error than simulation for Mistral and Terra in every country. For DeepSeek, simulation has lower gap error but direct estimation has lower group-mean error; these comparisons depend on low-validity older-adult simulation cells. Evaluating LLM outputs as proxies for age groups within countries therefore requires checking both group means and their contrast against human survey data.</p></details> |  |
| **[Automated structural testing of LLM-based agents: methods, framework, and case studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18827v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents are rapidly being adopted across diverse domains. Since they interact with users without supervision, they must be tested extensively. Current testing approaches focus on acceptance-level evaluation from the user's perspective. While intuitive, these tests require manual evaluation, are difficult to automate, do not facilitate root cause analysis, and incur expensive test environments. In this paper, we present methods to enable structural testing of LLM-based agents. Our approach utilizes traces (based on OpenTelemetry) to capture agent trajectories, employs mocking to enforce reproducible LLM behavior, and adds assertions to automate test verification. This enables testing agent components and interactions at a deeper technical level within automated workflows. We demonstrate how structural testing enables the adaptation of software engineering best practices to agents, including the test automation pyramid, regression testing, test-driven development, and multi-language testing. In representative case studies, we demonstrate automated execution and faster root-cause analysis. Collectively, these methods reduce testing costs and improve agent quality through higher coverage, reusability, and earlier defect detection. We provide an open source reference implementation on GitHub.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 5 figures. Accepted for publication in the 2025 IEEE International Conference on BigData 2025 (main track). Copyright (c) 2025 IEEE. Source code for the introduced methods and framework available at https://github.com/awslabs/generative-ai-toolkit</p></details> |
| **[Toollery: Scaling LLM Agents to Thousands of Skills and Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22218v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM agents are exposed to hundreds to tens of thousands of skills, tools, and API functions, full-library prompting becomes costly, slow, and less reliable: each added candidate increases prompt tokens and latency, while longer candidate lists introduce more distractors for LLM selection. We present Toollery, a training-free candidate-compression framework for scalable LLM skill/tool selection. Following established document-side query expansion, Toollery generates user-intent queries from each skill/tool specification and builds a retrieval index that maps real user requests to compact candidate sets before final LLM decision-making. By treating high-level skills and atomic tools as selectable capabilities, Toollery can be applied to both skill libraries and tool registries. We evaluate Toollery on the roughly 79K-capability SkillRouter benchmark, BFCL-V4 with over 440 atomic tools, and 3,396 proprietary smart-cockpit requests over 220 tools. Across these settings, Toollery keeps online selection bounded to a compact top-K candidate set and improves recall over ordinary specification retrieval. At a fixed top-10 budget, Toollery improves end-to-end selection on the cockpit dataset, and maintains comparable AST Accuracy on BFCL-V4. These results support Toollery as a practical candidate-compression framework for large and evolving agent capability libraries, while showing that quality and cost gains depend on workload coverage and provider caching.</p></details> | <details><summary>To be...</summary><p>To be presented at the 2026 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP 2026)</p></details> |
| **[MemBubble: Benchmarking How Agent Memory Shapes LLM Response Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25127v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory serves as the core component of LLM-based agents, enabling personalized responses via extracting and using user preferences from past interactions. However, its use may reduce response diversity, thereby narrowing the range of information available to users. Existing evaluations rely largely on synthetic interactions and cover few memory systems, leaving the effects of memory on response diversity insufficiently evaluated in real-world scenarios. We introduce MemBubble, a benchmark of 1000 instances built on preferences extracted from real user-assistant conversations. To measure the extent to which memory concentrates response distributions, we construct test queries in scenarios distinct from user preferences while preserving topical relevance through shared preference categories. Across multiple LLMs and memory mechanisms, entropy decreases by 34.7% on average compared with directly providing the full interaction history, highlighting the important role of memory processing in diversity loss. We also explore simple user-side interventions and changes in information availability but find that these do not effectively close the diversity gap. Our findings highlight the need to consider preserving diversity and user access to information alongside improvements in personalization when designing and using agent memory systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Empowering Edge Agents: A Systematic Analysis of Edge-Cloud Collaboration in LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17816v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. Code and resources are available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/EC2-Bench.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 5 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[ClinSDT: LLM-Encoded Clinical Semantic Guidance for Sepsis Treatment via Decision Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25121v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. Decision Transformer-based methods provide a natural sequence modeling framework for this task, but they mainly rely on raw physiological states and historical clinician actions. Such raw trajectory tokens may not explicitly capture clinical semantics that clinicians use in practice, such as patient severity, recent treatment response, and current risk priorities. To address this limitation, we propose ClinSDT, an LLM-assisted Decision Transformer framework for offline sepsis treatment. ClinSDT uses a frozen LLM as a semantic bridge rather than a treatment decision maker. It constructs three clinical semantic views from ICU trajectories, namely Profile, Context, and Advisory, and inserts their encoded representations as independent tokens into a Decision Transformer policy. To support policy refinement and rollout, ClinSDT further trains a Treatment-Response Forecaster to estimate the next physiological state, SAPS-II change, and predictive uncertainty from state-action inputs. In the second training stage, the forecaster screens local counterfactual treatment candidates, and only low-uncertainty candidates with lower predicted SAPS-II increase are distilled into the policy after semantic regeneration. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that ClinSDT achieves consistent relative improvements over representative imitation learning, offline reinforcement learning, and Decision Transformer baselines under the same rollout protocol. Code and data are available at https://github.com/KAG778/ClinSDT.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 6 figures. Accepted at CIKM 2026 (Full Research Track, Oral Presentation)</p></details> |
| **[Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.</p></details> |  |
